Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and D.
2.  **Identify direct connections to the target node D:** I observe that node D is directly connected only to nodes J and P.
3.  **Analyze the implications of these connections:** Any path from node E to node D must, by definition, pass through either node J or node P as its last step before reaching D.
4.  **Consider removing J and P:** If both node J and node P are removed, node D will have no remaining connections to any other node in the graph. Consequently, no path from E (or any other node) to D can exist.
5.  **Check if J and P can be removed:** The rules state that only the two black nodes (E and D) cannot be removed. J and P are not black nodes, so they can be removed.
6.  **Evaluate minimality:**
    *   If only J is removed, paths from E to D can still exist through P (e.g., E -> B -> C -> O -> P -> D).
    *   If only P is removed, paths from E to D can still exist through J (e.g., E -> B -> H -> I -> J -> D).
    *   Therefore, removing a single node (either J or P) is not sufficient to disconnect E and D.
7.  **Conclusion:** Since a single node removal is insufficient, and removing J and P (two nodes) successfully disconnects E and D, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [J, P]
--- Ground Truth ---

Image name: PG_task3_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are J (black) and F (black). The task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect J and F.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node J is connected to: M, D, P, I.
    *   Node F is connected to: B, E, L, X, K, G.

2.  **Strategy: Find edge-disjoint paths.**
    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes (the edge-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. If we can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least *k*. If we then find a cut set of size *k*, that is our answer.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths from J to F:**
    Let's systematically find paths, ensuring that each new path uses edges not used by previous paths.

    *   **Path 1:** J - M - L - F
        Edges: (J, M), (M, L), (L, F)

    *   **Path 2:** J - D - P - O - B - F
        Edges: (J, D), (D, P), (P, O), (O, B), (B, F)
        (Check for disjointness with Path 1: All edges are new.)

    *   **Path 3:** J - I - H - X - F
        Edges: (J, I), (I, H), (H, X), (X, F)
        (Check for disjointness with Path 1 and Path 2: All edges are new.)

    *   **Path 4:** J - P - Y - N - B - E - F
        Edges: (J, P), (P, Y), (Y, N), (N, B), (B, E), (E, F)
        (Check for disjointness with Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3: All edges are new.)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    Let's list all edges used in these four paths:
    *   Path 1: (J, M), (M, L), (L, F)
    *   Path 2: (J, D), (D, P), (P, O), (O, B), (B, F)
    *   Path 3: (J, I), (I, H), (H, X), (X, F)
    *   Path 4: (J, P), (P, Y), (Y, N), (N, B), (B, E), (E, F)

    Upon inspection, all 17 listed edges are unique to their respective paths. Therefore, we have successfully identified 4 edge-disjoint paths between J and F.

5.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**
    Since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths, Menger's Theorem states that at least 4 edges must be removed to disconnect J and F.

6.  **Find a cut set of size 4:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
    Node J is connected to M, D, P, and I.
    If we remove the edges (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), and (J, I), node J will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph. This means no path can exist between J and F.
    This cut set has 4 edges.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    We have found 4 edge-disjoint paths, meaning the minimum cut is at least 4. We have also found a cut set of 4 edges that successfully disconnects J from F. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['G', 'H']]}

  - Queue for next level: [E, H, I, O]
  - Visited nodes: {N, B, C, Y}

- **Level 3:** Neighbors of nodes from Level 2 (excluding already visited nodes).
  - From E:

    - H is connected to G. **Target node G found!** Path: [N, B, H, G]. This path also has a length of 3.
  - From I:
    - I is connected to J. Path: [N, C, I, J]
    - I is connected to P. Path: [N, C, I, P]
  - From O:
    - O is connected to P. Path: [N, C, O, P]

Since the target node G was found at Level 3, the shortest path length is 3. Both [N, B, E, G] and [N, B, H, G] are valid shortest paths. I will provide the first one found.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are M and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect M from B. I cannot remove M or B.

First, I identify all possible paths from M to B. I can categorize these paths based on the first node they visit from M:

**Paths starting from M through L:**
1.  M - L - E - B
2.  M - L - E - G - B

**Paths starting from M through F:**
3.  M - F - E - B
4.  M - F - E - G - B
5.  M - F - K - G - B
6.  M - F - X - K - G - B

**Paths starting from M through J:**
7.  M - J - I - C - B
8.  M - J - I - O - C - B
9.  M - J - P - O - C - B
10. M - J - P - O - N - B

Next, I attempt to find a minimum vertex cut.

**1. Check for a cut set of size 1:**
No single node appears to be an articulation point that disconnects M and B, as there are clearly multiple independent routes. For example, removing J would still leave paths through L and F. Removing E would still leave paths through G, I, and P.

**2. Check for a cut set of size 2:**
I systematically test combinations of two nodes:
*   **[E, G]**: This would disconnect M from B via paths through L and F, but paths through J would remain (e.g., M-J-I-C-B).
*   **[I, P]**: This would disconnect M from B via paths through J, but paths through L and F would remain (e.g., M-L-E-B).
*   **[J, E]**: Removing J cuts all paths through J. Removing E cuts paths M-L-E-B, M-L-E-G-B, M-F-E-B, M-F-E-G-B. However, paths like M-F-K-G-B and M-F-X-K-G-B would still exist.
*   **[J, G]**: Removing J cuts all paths through J. Removing G cuts paths M-L-E-G-B, M-F-E-G-B, M-F-K-G-B, M-F-X-K-G-B. However, paths like M-L-E-B and M-F-E-B would still exist.
*   **[L, F]**: Removing L and F would cut paths M-L-... and M-F-..., but paths through J would remain (e.g., M-J-I-C-B).
*   **[L, J]**: Removing L and J would cut paths M-L-... and M-J-..., but paths through F would remain (e.g., M-F-E-B).
*   **[F, J]**: Removing F and J would cut paths M-F-... and M-J-..., but paths through L would remain (e.g., M-L-E-B).
Based on this, a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient.

**3. Check for a cut set of size 3:**
I consider nodes that act as "bottlenecks" for major branches of paths.
*   **Option 1: Remove the initial branching nodes from M.**
    The three main branches from M are through L, F, and J. If I remove all three of these nodes, M will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B.
    *   Removing L breaks paths M-L-E-B and M-L-E-G-B.
    *   Removing F breaks paths M-F-E-B, M-F-E-G-B, M-F-K-G-B, M-F-X-K-G-B.
    *   Removing J breaks paths M-J-I-C-B, M-J-I-O-C-B, M-J-P-O-C-B, M-J-P-O-N-B.
    Therefore, **[L, F, J]** is a valid cut set of size 3.

*   **Option 2: Remove a combination of nodes that cover all path groups.**
    Consider the paths from M to B. They can be broadly divided into two main groups:
    *   **Group A (Left side):** Paths through L or F. All these paths eventually pass through either E or G to reach B. Thus, removing **E and G** will disconnect M from B via this group of paths.
    *   **Group B (Right side):** Paths through J. All these paths eventually pass through either I or P to reach the C/O/N cluster, which then connects to B. Thus, removing **I and P** will disconnect M from B via this group of paths.
    If I remove [E, G, I, P], M will be disconnected from B. This is a cut set of size 4. This is not minimal.

    Let's try to combine one node from M's immediate neighbors with two nodes from B's "entry points" for the other paths.
    Consider removing **J**. This breaks all paths in Group B.
    Now, I only need to disconnect M from B via Group A paths (through L or F). As established, removing **E and G** will achieve this.
    Therefore, **[J, E, G]** is a valid cut set of size 3.

Since I have found multiple cut sets of size 3 and confirmed that no cut set of size 2 exists, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [J, E, G]

2.  **Analyze Connections from H**: H is directly connected to G, F, E, I, B.
3.  **Analyze Connections to N**: N is directly connected to B, C, O, Y.
4.  **Identify Potential Bottlenecks**: To disconnect H and N, all paths between them must be broken. I will look for "choke points" or sets of nodes that all paths must pass through.
    *   Any path from H to N must pass through one of H's neighbors (G, F, E, I, B) and one of N's neighbors (B, C, O, Y).
    *   Notice that nodes G and F are primarily connected to H and then to E (e.g., H-G-E, H-F-E). So, paths through G or F will eventually rely on E to reach the "right" side of the graph towards N.
    *   Therefore, all paths from H to N must pass through at least one of the nodes B, I, or E. This suggests that {B, I, E} could be a cut set.
5.  **Find Node-Disjoint Paths**: To determine the minimum number of nodes required, I will look for node-disjoint paths between H and N. If I can find 'k' paths that do not share any intermediate nodes, then at least 'k' nodes must be removed.
    *   **Path 1**: H - B - N
        *   Intermediate node: {B}
    *   **Path 2**: H - I - C - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {I, C}
    *   **Path 3**: H - E - M - J - P - O - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {E, M, J, P, O}
6.  **Verify Node-Disjoint Property**:
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {B}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {I, C}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {E, M, J, P, O}.
    *   These three sets are mutually exclusive (they share no common nodes). Therefore, these are three node-disjoint paths between H and N.
7.  **Apply Menger's Theorem**: Since there are 3 node-disjoint paths between H and N, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect them.
8.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3**: The set {B, I, E} contains one node from each of the identified node-disjoint paths (B from Path 1, I from Path 2, E from Path 3).
9.  **Verify Disconnection with Cut Set [B, I, E]**:
    *   If nodes B, I, and E are removed:
        *   H is now only connected to G and F.
        *   N is now only connected to C, O, and Y.
        *   The nodes G, F, X, K are only connected to H (and each other). They have no remaining connections to the part of the graph containing N.
        *   The nodes C, O, Y, P, J, M, L, D are only connected to N (and each other). They have no remaining connections to the part of the graph containing H.
        *   Therefore, H and N are completely disconnected.
10. **Conclusion**: Since we found 3 node-disjoint paths, and we identified a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects H and N, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, E]

- Neighbors of J: D, P, I, M

Let's try to find vertex-disjoint paths from L to J. If we can find 'k' vertex-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, at least 'k' nodes must be removed to disconnect L and J.

1.  **Path 1: L - M - J**
    This is a direct path through node M.
    Nodes in this path (excluding L and J): {M}

- If I remove F, all paths starting with L-F-... are severed.

- Removing M: Cuts Path 1 (L-M-J) and any other path through M (e.g., L-M-P-J).

    *   **Can 2 edges disconnect E and H?** To determine this, I will look for edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least *k*.

    Since I found a cut set of 3 edges [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)] that successfully disconnects E from H, and I've determined that at least 3 edges are required, this cut set is indeed minimal.

    *   Neighbors of H: E, G, B, I. (G, K, X, F form a sub-graph connected to H via G, but G does not lead to O).
    *   Neighbors of O: C, N, P, Y. (Y does not lead to H).

    I will systematically trace paths from H to O.

    *   **Paths starting H -> E:**
        *   H -> E -> M -> J -> P -> O
        *   H -> E -> I -> C -> O
        *   H -> E -> I -> P -> O

    *   **Paths starting H -> B:**
        *   H -> B -> C -> O
        *   H -> B -> N -> O

    *   **Paths starting H -> I:**
        *   H -> I -> C -> O
        *   H -> I -> P -> O
        *   H -> I -> J -> P -> O

    *   (Paths starting H -> G do not lead to O, as G, K, X, F are isolated from the O side of the graph).

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size using Menger's Theorem (Vertex-Disjoint Paths):**
    To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between H and O. Vertex-disjoint paths share no intermediate nodes.

    Let's identify three vertex-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N - O (Intermediate nodes: B, N)
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - O (Intermediate nodes: I, C)
    *   **Path 3:** H - E - M - J - P - O (Intermediate nodes: E, M, J, P)

    Checking for disjointness:
    *   Intermediate nodes of Path 1: {B, N}
    *   Intermediate nodes of Path 2: {I, C}
    *   Intermediate nodes of Path 3: {E, M, J, P}

    All sets of intermediate nodes are distinct. Therefore, these three paths are vertex-disjoint.
    According to Menger's Theorem, if there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths, then at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect H and O. This means the minimum cut set size is at least 3.

5.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects H and O.
    Consider the nodes directly connected to O: C, N, P, Y. Node Y is not connected to any path leading from H. Therefore, all paths from H to O must pass through C, N, or P.
    If I remove nodes C, N, and P:
    *   O will no longer have any connections to the rest of the graph that leads to H.
    *   All paths listed in step 3 (which all end in C, N, or P before reaching O) will be broken.

    Therefore, removing the set {C, N, P} will completely disconnect H from O.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3. I have also identified a cut set of size 3 ({C, N, P}) that successfully disconnects H and O. Thus, 3 is the minimum number of nodes.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, N, P]

        *   Visited: `{..., X, G}`.

        *   Neighbors of B: H, C, N (E is already visited).
        *   Enqueue `(H, [E, B, H])`, `(C, [E, B, C])`, `(N, [E, B, N])`.
        *   Visited: `{..., H, C, N}`.
    *   Queue: `[(J, [E, M, J]), (X, [E, F, X]), (G, [E, F, G]), (H, [E, B, H]), (C, [E, B, C]), (N, [E, B, N])]`

        *   Neighbors of J: P (M is visited).
        *   Enqueue `(P, [E, M, J, P])`.
        *   Visited: `{..., P}`.

        *   Neighbors of X: K, G (F is visited, G is visited).
        *   Enqueue `(K, [E, F, X, K])`.

        *   Node I is the target! Path found: `[E, B, H, I]`. The length is 3.
        *   Since this is a BFS, the first path found to the target is guaranteed to be the shortest.

I verify the path:
*   Is E directly connected to B? Yes.
*   Is B directly connected to H? Yes.
*   Is H directly connected to I? Yes.

The path `[E, B, H, I]` is valid and has a length of 3. No shorter path can exist because BFS explores level by level.

Length: 3, Path: [E, B, H, I]

Image name: FD-K_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are G and N. My goal is to disconnect G from N by removing the minimum number of white nodes. I cannot remove G or N.

2.  **Analyze connections from G:** I observe that node G is directly connected to only two other nodes: F and H. Any path from G to N must therefore start with either G-F-... or G-H-....

3.  **Consider a single-node removal:**
    *   If I remove node F, paths starting G-F-... are broken. However, paths starting G-H-... still exist (e.g., G-H-B-N, G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-P-O-N, G-H-I-J-P-O-N). Thus, removing F alone does not disconnect G from N.
    *   If I remove node H, paths starting G-H-... are broken. However, paths starting G-F-... still exist (e.g., G-F-E-M-J-P-O-N, G-F-E-H-B-N (this path is broken if H is removed, but G-F-E-M-J-P-O-N is still valid)). Thus, removing H alone does not disconnect G from N.
    *   I can systematically check every other white node (E, B, C, I, O, P, J, M, L, D, K, X, Y). For example, if I remove E, paths like G-H-B-N still exist. If I remove I, paths like G-H-B-N still exist. If I remove B, paths like G-H-I-C-N still exist. If I remove C, paths like G-H-B-N still exist. If I remove O, paths like G-H-B-N still exist. If I remove P, paths like G-H-B-N still exist. If I remove J, paths like G-H-B-N still exist. In all cases, at least one path from G to N remains.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient to disconnect G and N. This means the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

4.  **Consider a two-node removal:**
    *   Since all paths from G must pass through either F or H, removing both F and H would isolate G from the rest of the graph.
    *   If F is removed, G can no longer use the G-F-... paths.
    *   If H is removed, G can no longer use the G-H-... paths.
    *   With both F and H removed, G has no remaining connections to any other white node, and therefore no path to N.
    *   This set of nodes [F, H] successfully disconnects G from N.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since a single node removal is not sufficient, and we have found a 2-node cut set, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [F, H]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['E', 'H'], ['F', 'H']]}

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from H.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node E is connected to: L, M, F, G, B.
    *   Node H is connected to: G, I, B.

2.  **Consider the edges directly connected to H:**
    Any path from E to H must eventually use one of the edges connected to H. These edges are (G, H), (I, H), and (B, H). If we remove all three of these edges, node H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from E. This would be a cut set of size 3: `[(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]`.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:**
    To confirm if 3 is the minimum, I will try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum cut size is at least *k*.

    Let's identify some edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - G - H
        Edges: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`
    *   **Path 2:** E - B - H
        Edges: `(E, B)`, `(B, H)`
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I - H
        Edges: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, I)`, `(I, H)`

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    Let's check if these three paths share any common edges:
    *   Edges of Path 1: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`
    *   Edges of Path 2: `(E, B)`, `(B, H)`
    *   Edges of Path 3: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, I)`, `(I, H)`

    All edges listed for Path 1 are distinct from those in Path 2 and Path 3.
    All edges listed for Path 2 are distinct from those in Path 1 and Path 3.
    All edges listed for Path 3 are distinct from those in Path 1 and Path 2.
    Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Determine the minimum cut:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.
    As identified in step 2, the cut set `[(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]` has a size of 3 and successfully disconnects H from all other nodes, including E.

    Since we found a cut set of size 3 and established that the minimum cut must be at least 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---

3.  **Analyze connections to the target node D:** Node D is directly connected to four other nodes: J, P, O, and Y. This means any path from F to D must pass through one of the edges (J, D), (P, D), (O, D), or (Y, D).
4.  **Propose an initial cut set:** A straightforward way to disconnect D from the rest of the graph (and thus from F) is to remove all edges directly connected to D. This cut set would be: [(J, D), (P, D), (O, D), (Y, D)]. This set contains 4 edges.
5.  **Determine if this cut set is minimal:** To prove that 4 is the minimum number of edges, we need to find at least 4 edge-disjoint paths between F and D. If we can find 4 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum cut size must be at least 4. Since we already found a cut set of size 4, it would be the minimum.
6.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths from F to D:**
    *   **Path 1:** F - M - J - D
        Edges: (F, M), (M, J), (J, D)
    *   **Path 2:** F - K - I - P - D
        Edges: (F, K), (K, I), (I, P), (P, D)
    *   **Path 3:** F - E - B - C - O - D
        Edges: (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, D)
    *   **Path 4:** F - G - B - N - Y - D
        Edges: (F, G), (G, B), (B, N), (N, Y), (Y, D)
7.  **Verify edge-disjointness:** Let's list all edges used in these four paths and check for overlaps:
    *   From F: (F, M), (F, K), (F, E), (F, G) - All distinct.
    *   To D: (J, D), (P, D), (O, D), (Y, D) - All distinct.
    *   Intermediate edges: (M, J), (K, I), (I, P), (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (G, B), (B, N), (N, Y) - All distinct from each other and from the F-start and D-end edges.
    Since all edges in these four paths are unique, these paths are indeed edge-disjoint.
8.  **Conclusion:** We have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between F and D. Therefore, according to Menger's theorem, at least 4 edges must be removed to disconnect F from D. The cut set identified in step 4, [(J, D), (P, D), (O, D), (Y, D)], consists of exactly 4 edges and successfully disconnects D from the rest of the graph. This confirms that 4 is the minimum number of edges.

Image name: FD-FR_task2_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are G and N. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect G from N. I cannot remove G or N.

2.  **Analyze connections from G:**
    *   G is directly connected to X, K, F, and H.

3.  **Analyze connections from N:**
    *   N is directly connected to B, C, O, and Y.

4.  **Systematically explore paths and identify potential cut nodes:**
    Let's consider all possible paths from G to N. I'll try to identify "bottleneck" nodes or sets of nodes that all paths must traverse.

    *   **Paths starting from G through H:**
        *   G-H-B-N
        *   G-H-B-C-N
        *   G-H-B-I-C-N
        *   G-H-B-I-J-P-O-N
        *   G-H-I-C-N
        *   G-H-I-J-P-O-N
        *   G-H-E-I-C-N
        *   G-H-E-I-J-P-O-N
        *   G-H-E-L-M-J-P-O-N

    *   **Paths starting from G through F (or K or X, which lead to F):**
        *   G-F-E-I-C-N
        *   G-F-E-I-J-P-O-N
        *   G-F-E-L-M-J-P-O-N
        *   (Similar paths exist if starting G-K-F-E... or G-X-K-F-E...)

5.  **Identify critical nodes for disconnection:**
    Let's consider the immediate neighbors of G: {X, K, F, H}.
    Any path from G to N must pass through at least one of these nodes.

    *   If a path goes through H (e.g., G-H-B-N), it uses H.
    *   If a path does *not* go through H, it must go through X, K, or F.
        *   X is connected to K, F.
        *   K is connected to X, F.
        *   F is connected to X, K, E.
        Therefore, any path from G that does not use H must eventually pass through F. From F, the only way to reach the "right" side of the graph (towards N) is through node E.

    This means that every single path from G to N must pass through either node H OR node E.

6.  **Test the identified cut set:**
    *   If I remove only H: Paths like G-F-E-I-C-N still exist. So H alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove only E: Paths like G-H-B-N still exist. So E alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove both H and E:
        *   All paths starting G-H-... are broken because H is removed.
        *   All paths starting G-X-K-F-E-... are broken because E is removed.
        *   All paths starting G-K-F-E-... are broken because E is removed.
        *   All paths starting G-F-E-... are broken because E is removed.

    After removing H and E, G is only connected to X, K, and F.
    Let's check if X, K, or F can reach N without H or E:
    *   X is connected to K, F.
    *   K is connected to X, F.
    *   F is connected to X, K.
    None of X, K, or F are connected to any node that leads to N (B, C, O, Y, I, J, P, L, M, D) without passing through H or E. The only connection F has to the "middle" of the graph is via E, which is removed.

    Therefore, removing both H and E successfully disconnects G from N.

7.  **Determine minimality:**
    Since a single node removal (H or E) was not sufficient, and we found a set of two nodes that disconnects G and N, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, E]

1.  M-L-E-B
2.  M-L-E-G-B

**Group 2: Paths starting with M-E**
3.  M-E-B
4.  M-E-G-B

**Group 3: Paths starting with M-F**
5.  M-F-G-B
6.  M-F-X-K-G-B
7.  M-F-K-G-B

**Group 4: Paths starting with M-J**
8.  M-J-I-H-B
9.  M-J-I-C-B
10. M-J-P-O-C-B
11. M-J-P-O-N-B

Next, I will try to find a minimum cut set of edges. I'll start by considering small numbers of edges.

**Attempt to find a cut of size 1:**
No single edge can disconnect M from B, as there are multiple distinct "branches" of paths (e.g., through E, F, and J). For example, removing (E,B) would still leave paths through G and J.

**Attempt to find a cut of size 2:**
Let's consider removing two edges.
- If I remove (E,B) and (G,B):
    - Paths M-E-B, M-L-E-B are cut by (E,B).
    - Paths M-E-G-B, M-L-E-G-B, M-F-G-B, M-F-X-K-G-B, M-F-K-G-B are cut by (G,B).
    - However, all paths starting with M-J (paths 8-11) remain open. So, this is not a complete cut.
- If I remove (M,E) and (M,J):
    - Paths M-E-B, M-E-G-B are cut by (M,E).
    - Paths 8-11 are cut by (M,J).
    - However, paths M-L-E-B, M-L-E-G-B (via L) and paths 5-7 (via F) remain open. So, this is not a complete cut.

Since a cut of size 2 is not sufficient, the minimum number of edges must be at least 3. This is also supported by Menger's theorem, as I can identify at least 3 edge-disjoint paths:
1.  P1: M-E-B (edges: (M,E), (E,B))
2.  P2: M-L-E-G-B (edges: (M,L), (L,E), (E,G), (G,B))
3.  P3: M-J-I-H-B (edges: (M,J), (J,I), (I,H), (H,B))
These three paths do not share any edges. Therefore, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect M and B.

**Attempt to find a cut of size 3:**
I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal breaks all 11 identified paths.
Let's consider the following set of edges: [(E,B), (G,B), (M,J)].

Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects M from B:
1.  **Remove (E,B):**
    *   This directly cuts Path 1 (M-L-E-B) and Path 3 (M-E-B).
2.  **Remove (G,B):**
    *   This directly cuts Path 2 (M-L-E-G-B), Path 4 (M-E-G-B), Path 5 (M-F-G-B), Path 6 (M-F-X-K-G-B), and Path 7 (M-F-K-G-B).
    *   At this point, all paths from M that go through L, E, or F to reach B are broken.
3.  **Remove (M,J):**
    *   This cuts the very first edge of all paths in Group 4 (paths 8-11).
    *   Specifically, Path 8 (M-J-I-H-B), Path 9 (M-J-I-C-B), Path 10 (M-J-P-O-C-B), and Path 11 (M-J-P-O-N-B) are all broken.

After removing these three edges, no path remains between M and B. This set of 3 edges successfully disconnects the two black nodes. Since we established that at least 3 edges are required, this set is minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, B), (G, B), (M, J)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F (black) and J (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F from J.

1.  **Identify all edges connected to the target nodes:**
    *   Edges connected to F: (F,X), (F,K), (F,G), (F,E)
    *   Edges connected to J: (J,M), (J,I), (J,P)

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    A straightforward approach to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges incident to it.
    *   If we remove all edges connected to F, we would remove 4 edges: (F,X), (F,K), (F,G), (F,E). This would disconnect F from J.
    *   If we remove all edges connected to J, we would remove 3 edges: (J,M), (J,I), (J,P). This would disconnect J from F.
    Since 3 is less than 4, removing the edges connected to J is a better candidate for the minimum cut. This establishes an upper bound of 3 for the minimum number of edges.

3.  **Determine if a cut of size 1 or 2 is possible:**
    *   **Cut of size 1:** It's clear that removing any single edge will not disconnect F from J, as there are multiple distinct paths. For example, removing (J,M) still leaves paths like F-G-H-I-J and F-E-B-C-O-P-J.
    *   **Cut of size 2:** Let's assume we can disconnect F and J by removing only two edges. This implies that there are at most two edge-disjoint paths between F and J. Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths, which would prove that a cut of size 2 is impossible.

    Let's try to identify three paths from F to J that are as edge-disjoint as possible, especially at their ends near J:
    *   **Path 1 (via M):** F-E-M-J. Edges: {(F,E), (E,M), (M,J)}
    *   **Path 2 (via I):** F-G-H-I-J. Edges: {(F,G), (G,H), (H,I), (I,J)}
    *   **Path 3 (via P):** We need a path from F to P that does not use any edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   Path 1 uses (F,E). Path 2 uses (F,G).
        *   So, Path 3 must start with (F,X) or (F,K).
        *   If we start with (F,X):
            *   F-X-K. (K,M) is available, but (M,J) is used by Path 1.
            *   F-X-G. (X,G) is available, but (G,H) and subsequent edges are used by Path 2.
        *   If we start with (F,K):
            *   F-K-X. (X,G) is available, but (G,H) and subsequent edges are used by Path 2.
            *   F-K-M. (K,M) is available, but (M,J) is used by Path 1.

    This attempt to find three *edge-disjoint* paths suggests that it might not be possible. However, Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths. If we can't find 3 edge-disjoint paths, it doesn't automatically mean the cut is 2. We need to prove that 2 edges are insufficient.

    Let's consider the edges directly connected to J: (J,M), (J,I), (J,P).
    If we remove any two of these edges, the third edge still provides a path to J.
    *   If we remove (J,M) and (J,I): Paths to J must now go through (J,P).
        *   Example path: F-E-B-C-O-P-J. This path is still intact.
        *   Example path: F-G-H-I-P-J. This path is still intact (I is connected to P, and P to J).
        *   Example path: F-X-G-H-I-P-J. This path is still intact.
        *   Example path: F-K-X-G-H-I-P-J. This path is still intact.
        Since multiple paths to J via P still exist, removing (J,M) and (J,I) is not sufficient.

    *   Similarly, if we remove (J,M) and (J,P), paths to J via (J,I) would still exist (e.g., F-G-H-I-J).
    *   If we remove (J,I) and (J,P), paths to J via (J,M) would still exist (e.g., F-E-M-J).

    Therefore, removing any two edges is not enough to disconnect F from J. This proves that the minimum cut size must be at least 3.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we established that a cut of size 2 is not sufficient, and we found a cut of size 3 (by removing all edges incident to J), the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   Can we achieve disconnection with only 1 edge? No. If only (J, D) is removed, paths like F-...-P-D could still exist. If only (P, D) is removed, paths like F-...-J-D could still exist. Therefore, 1 edge is not sufficient.

    *   Upon careful inspection, it is clear that all paths from F to J must pass through either the edge (M, J) or the edge (I, J).

1.  **Removing (F, P)**: Cuts all paths starting F-P.
2.  **Removing (F, G)**: Cuts all paths starting F-G. Additionally, paths starting F-K (e.g., F-K-G-H-I-J) and F-X (e.g., F-X-G-H-I-J) are also cut because K and X both connect to G, and (F,G) is the only way for F to reach G via K or X.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from B.

1.  **Identify direct connections to target nodes:**
    *   Node H is connected to: I, G, X.
    *   Node B is connected to: C, E, N, O.

2.  **Consider simple cut strategies:**
    *   **Isolate H:** Removing all edges connected to H would disconnect it from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. The edges are (H, I), (H, G), and (H, X). This is a cut of size 3.
    *   **Isolate B:** Removing all edges connected to B would disconnect it. The edges are (B, C), (B, E), (B, N), and (B, O). This is a cut of size 4.
    *   A cut of size 3 is better than a cut of size 4. So, the minimum is at most 3.

3.  **Check for a cut of size 1:**
    *   A cut of size 1 would mean there's a bridge edge whose removal disconnects H and B.
    *   If we remove any single edge connected to H (e.g., (H, I)), H can still reach the rest of the graph via (H, G) or (H, X).
    *   If we remove any single edge connected to B (e.g., (B, E)), B can still be reached via (B, C), (B, N), or (B, O).
    *   Therefore, no cut of size 1 is possible.

4.  **Check for a cut of size 2:**
    *   To prove that a cut of size 2 is impossible, I need to find at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B (Menger's Theorem).
    *   Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths:
        *   **Path 1:** H - I - C - B
            *   Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, B)
        *   **Path 2:** H - X - F - E - B
            *   Edges: (H, X), (X, F), (F, E), (E, B)
        *   **Path 3:** H - G - I - J - P - O - B
            *   Edges: (H, G), (G, I), (I, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, B)

    *   **Verify edge-disjointness:**
        *   Edges in Path 1: (H, I), (I, C), (C, B)
        *   Edges in Path 2: (H, X), (X, F), (F, E), (E, B)
        *   Edges in Path 3: (H, G), (G, I), (I, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, B)
        *   By inspecting the edge lists, no edge appears in more than one path. For example, (H,I) is only in Path 1, (H,X) is only in Path 2, and (H,G) is only in Path 3. Similarly for all other edges.
        *   Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    *   Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them must be at least 3.
    *   We already identified a cut set of size 3: [(H, I), (H, G), (H, X)]. This set isolates H, thus breaking all paths to B.

6.  **Final verification:**
    *   Removing [(H, I), (H, G), (H, X)] completely isolates node H. No path can originate from H to reach B.
    *   No smaller set of edges (1 or 2) can disconnect H and B, as proven by the existence of 3 edge-disjoint paths.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, I), (H, G), (H, X)]

4.  **Remove {H, I, P}:**
    *   Path G-F-E-B-N remains intact (it uses F, E, B, none of which are H, I, or P).

Given the structure of the graph, these four nodes represent distinct "bottlenecks" or "branches" that must all be severed.

*   The path originating from J through M (J -> M -> L -> E -> F) passes through E.
*   All paths originating from J through I (J -> I -> H -> ...) pass through H. If they then go to E (J -> I -> H -> C -> B -> E -> F), they still pass through H first.

*   If I remove H: Paths like J -> M -> L -> E -> F would still exist.
*   If I remove any other single node (e.g., B, C, N, O, P, D, M, I, L, K, X, G, Y), there would always be at least one path remaining that does not use the removed node. For example, removing B would leave J -> M -> L -> E -> F and J -> I -> H -> F.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are G and I. The task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between G and I:**
    To systematically approach this, I will look for edge-disjoint paths. Menger's theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect them.

2.  **Find edge-disjoint paths from G to I:**
    *   **Path 1:** G - E - I
        Edges: {(G, E), (E, I)}
    *   **Path 2:** G - H - I
        Edges: {(G, H), (H, I)}
        (Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint as all four edges are distinct.)
    *   **Path 3:** G - F - E - M - J - I
        Edges: {(G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)}
        Let's check if Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
        -   Edges of Path 1: (G, E), (E, I)
        -   Edges of Path 2: (G, H), (H, I)
        -   Edges of Path 3: (G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)
        All edges in these three paths are distinct. For example, (G,E) is not (G,F) or (F,E), and (E,I) is not (E,M) or (J,I). Similarly for other edges.

3.  **Determine the lower bound for the cut:**
    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and I, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G from I is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges:**
    Now, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal will disconnect G from I.
    Consider the nodes directly connected to G: K, X, F, E, H.
    Any path from G to I must eventually pass through E or H, or through F which then connects to E.
    Let's consider the edges that connect the "left side" of the graph (containing G, K, X, F) to the "right side" (containing I and its connections).
    The edges connecting the set {G, K, X, F} to the rest of the graph (which includes E and H, and eventually I) are:
    *   (G, E)
    *   (G, H)
    *   (F, E)

5.  **Verify the cut set:**
    If we remove these three edges:
    *   **(G, E)**: This breaks all direct paths from G to E, such as G-E-I, G-E-M-J-I, etc.
    *   **(G, H)**: This breaks all direct paths from G to H, such as G-H-I, G-H-B-C-I, etc.
    *   **(F, E)**: This breaks all paths that go from G through F to E, such as G-F-E-I, G-F-E-M-J-I, G-F-E-H-I, etc.
    After removing these three edges, G (and its connected nodes K, X, F) cannot reach E or H. Since all paths from G to I must pass through E or H, G becomes completely disconnected from I.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    We have found a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects G from I, and we have established that at least 3 edges are required. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Image name: FD-FR_task2_gt3_PAIR1

Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, N and P, I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent vertices (excluding the source and target) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

First, I identify the two black nodes: N and P.
Next, I will find as many vertex-disjoint paths as possible between N and P. Vertex-disjoint means that the paths share no intermediate nodes.

1.  **Path 1: N - O - P**
    This is a direct path through node O.
    Nodes used (excluding N and P): {O}

2.  **Path 2: N - C - I - P**
    This path uses nodes C and I.
    Checking for disjointness with Path 1: O is not C or I. So, this path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1.
    Nodes used (excluding N and P): {C, I}

3.  **Path 3: N - B - H - G - F - E - M - J - P**
    This path uses nodes B, H, G, F, E, M, J.
    Checking for disjointness with Path 1: O is not in {B, H, G, F, E, M, J}.
    Checking for disjointness with Path 2: C and I are not in {B, H, G, F, E, M, J}.
    So, this path is vertex-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.
    Nodes used (excluding N and P): {B, H, G, F, E, M, J}

I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between N and P. According to Menger's theorem, this means that at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect N and P.

Now, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects N and P. A natural choice for a cut set would be to pick one node from each of the vertex-disjoint paths found. The intermediate nodes in the paths are:
*   Path 1: {O}
*   Path 2: {C, I}
*   Path 3: {B, H, G, F, E, M, J}

A simpler approach for finding a cut set is to identify the "bottleneck" nodes that all paths must pass through.
Let's consider the immediate neighbors of N: {B, C, O, Y}.
Let's consider the immediate neighbors of P: {I, J, D, O}.

Notice that O is a common neighbor.
If we remove O, the direct path N-O-P and N-Y-O-P are broken.
After removing O, any remaining path from N to P must go through N's other neighbors (B, C, Y) and eventually reach P's other neighbors (I, J, D).
Specifically, if O is removed, P is only connected to I, J, and D.
Can N reach D without passing through I or J?
- N-B-H-G-F-E-M-J (J is a neighbor of P, so this path would be broken if J is removed)
- N-C-I (I is a neighbor of P, so this path would be broken if I is removed)
- N-Y-C-I (I is a neighbor of P, so this path would be broken if I is removed)

It appears that if O is removed, all remaining paths from N to P must pass through either I or J.
Therefore, if we remove the set of nodes {O, I, J}, all paths from N to P will be broken:
*   Paths through O (e.g., N-O-P, N-Y-O-P) are broken by removing O.
*   Paths through I (e.g., N-C-I-P, N-B-H-I-P, N-B-H-G-F-E-I-P, N-Y-C-I-P) are broken by removing I.
*   Paths through J (e.g., N-C-I-J-P, N-B-H-I-J-P, N-B-H-G-F-E-I-J-P, N-B-H-G-F-E-M-J-P, N-Y-C-I-J-P, N-Y-C-B-H-G-F-E-M-J-P) are broken by removing J.

Since we found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, and we have identified a cut set of size 3 ({O, I, J}), this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [O, I, J]

        If F is removed, all paths through F are broken, AND all paths through K (because K connects to X, which only connects to F) are also broken.

Node I is connected to: H, C, P.

Let's analyze the paths from G to I. All paths from G to I must eventually pass through one of the nodes directly connected to I (H, C, or P) and then use the edge connecting that node to I. Therefore, all paths from G to I must use one of the following three edges: (H, I), (C, I), or (P, I).

Let's list some example paths to illustrate this:
1.  **G - H - I**: This path uses edge (H, I).
2.  **G - F - E - B - H - I**: This path uses edge (H, I).
3.  **G - X - F - E - B - H - I**: This path uses edge (H, I).
4.  **G - F - E - B - C - I**: This path uses edge (C, I).
5.  **G - X - F - E - B - C - I**: This path uses edge (C, I).
6.  **G - F - E - B - C - O - P - I**: This path uses edge (P, I).
7.  **G - X - F - E - B - C - O - P - I**: This path uses edge (P, I).

To disconnect G from I, we must break all these paths. Since every path from G to I must use one of the edges (H, I), (C, I), or (P, I), removing these three edges will guarantee that I is completely isolated from G (and the rest of the graph).

Let's verify if a smaller number of edges would suffice:
*   **Can 1 edge disconnect G and I?** No. If we remove, for example, (H, I), paths like G - F - E - B - C - I or G - F - E - B - C - O - P - I would still exist.
*   **Can 2 edges disconnect G and I?** Let's try removing any two of the critical edges leading to I:
    *   If we remove (H, I) and (C, I), the path G - F - E - B - C - O - P - I (or G - X - F - E - B - C - O - P - I) still exists via (P, I).
    *   If we remove (H, I) and (P, I), the path G - F - E - B - C - I (or G - X - F - E - B - C - I) still exists via (C, I).
    *   If we remove (C, I) and (P, I), the path G - H - I (or G - F - E - B - H - I, etc.) still exists via (H, I).
    Since removing any two of these three edges leaves at least one path intact, 2 edges are not sufficient.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect G and I is 3. The set of edges [(H, I), (C, I), (P, I)] achieves this disconnection.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, I), (C, I), (P, I)]

First, I identify the immediate neighbors of G and I:
*   Neighbors of G: H, K, F, X
*   Neighbors of I: H, P, J, C

I will categorize all possible paths from G to I into main branches based on the first step from G. All paths from G must start with an edge to one of its neighbors: H, K, F, or X.
Notice that nodes K and X are both connected to F. Therefore, any path starting G-K or G-X will eventually pass through F to reach I. This means all paths from G can be grouped into two main categories:
1.  Paths starting with G-H.
2.  Paths starting with G-F (which includes paths like G-K-X-F... and G-X-F...).

Let's analyze these two main branches:

**Branch 1: Paths starting with G-H**
These paths include:
*   G - H - I (direct connection)
*   G - H - C - I
*   G - H - B - P - I
*   G - H - B - C - I
*   G - H - C - B - P - I
*   G - H - C - N - O - P - I
*   G - H - C - N - Y - O - P - I
To cut all paths in this branch, removing the edge (G, H) is sufficient. Once (G, H) is removed, G cannot reach H, and thus cannot reach I through any path that starts with G-H.

**Branch 2: Paths starting with G-F (including G-K-X-F... and G-X-F...)**
After removing (G, H), all remaining paths from G to I must go through F (since K and X lead to F). From F, there are two distinct ways to reach I:
*   **Sub-branch 2a: Through P**
    *   G - F - P - I
    *   G - K - X - F - P - I
    *   G - X - F - P - I
    To cut all paths in this sub-branch, removing the edge (P, I) is sufficient.
*   **Sub-branch 2b: Through E, M, J**
    *   G - F - E - M - J - I
    *   G - K - X - F - E - M - J - I
    *   G - X - F - E - M - J - I
    To cut all paths in this sub-branch, removing the edge (J, I) is sufficient.

Therefore, by removing the three edges:
1.  **(G, H)**: This cuts off all paths from G that go through H.
2.  **(P, I)**: This cuts off all paths from G that go through F and then P to reach I.
3.  **(J, I)**: This cuts off all paths from G that go through F and then E-M-J to reach I.

This set of three edges, [(G, H), (P, I), (J, I)], successfully disconnects G from I.

**Verifying Minimality:**
To confirm that 3 is the minimum number of edges, I consider if it's possible to disconnect G and I with only 2 edges.
Let's assume we remove any two edges.
Consider the three distinct "entry points" to I from G's perspective:
*   Via H (e.g., G-H-I, G-H-C-I)
*   Via P (e.g., G-F-P-I)
*   Via J (e.g., G-F-E-M-J-I)

If we remove only two edges, say e1 and e2:
*   If e1 cuts the H-branch (e.g., (G,H) or (H,I) or (H,C) and (C,I)), and e2 cuts the P-branch (e.g., (P,I) or (F,P)), the J-branch (G-F-E-M-J-I) would still remain.
*   If e1 cuts the H-branch and e2 cuts the J-branch, the P-branch would still remain.
*   If e1 cuts the P-branch and e2 cuts the J-branch, the H-branch would still remain.

More formally, consider the following three paths:
1.  Path 1: G - H - I
2.  Path 2: G - F - P - I
3.  Path 3: G - F - E - M - J - I

These three paths are "edge-independent" enough in their critical segments to require at least three cuts.
*   Path 1 uses (G,H) and (H,I).
*   Path 2 uses (F,P) and (P,I).
*   Path 3 uses (F,E), (E,M), (M,J), (J,I).
While Path 2 and Path 3 share the initial segment from G to F (e.g., (G,F)), the subsequent paths to I diverge and require separate cuts. To cut all paths, we need to cut at least one edge from each of these three distinct "channels" leading to I.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and J. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from J. I cannot remove E or J.

I will systematically identify all paths from E to J and then look for bottlenecks.

**1. Identify all distinct paths from E to J:**

*   **Path Group 1 (via M):**
    *   P1: E - M - J
    *   P2: E - L - M - J

*   **Path Group 2 (via F):**
    *   P3: E - F - X - K - G - H - I - J
    *   P4: E - F - K - G - H - I - J

*   **Path Group 3 (via B):**
    *   P5: E - B - N - Y - O - P - J
    *   P6: E - B - N - C - P - J
    *   P7: E - B - C - P - J
    *   P8: E - B - C - I - J
    *   P9: E - B - H - I - J

**2. Determine the minimum number of nodes to remove.**

To find the minimum number of nodes, I will try to find node-disjoint paths between E and J. By Menger's Theorem, the maximum number of node-disjoint paths is equal to the minimum number of nodes in a cut set.

Let's try to find three node-disjoint paths (excluding E and J):

*   **Path A:** E - M - J
    *   Intermediate nodes: {M}

*   **Path B:** E - F - K - G - H - I - J
    *   Intermediate nodes: {F, K, G, H, I}
    *   This path does not use M, so it is node-disjoint from Path A (except for E and J).

*   **Path C:** E - B - N - Y - O - P - J
    *   Intermediate nodes: {B, N, Y, O, P}
    *   This path does not use M, F, K, G, H, or I, so it is node-disjoint from Path A and Path B (except for E and J).

Since I have found three node-disjoint paths (A, B, C) between E and J, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E and J is at least 3.

**3. Identify a cut set of size 3.**

Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects E and J.
Based on the three node-disjoint paths identified:
*   Path A uses M.
*   Path B uses F.
*   Path C uses B.

If I remove nodes {M, F, B}, all three of these node-disjoint paths will be broken. Let's verify if this set breaks all other paths as well:

*   **Removing M:** Breaks P1 (E-M-J) and P2 (E-L-M-J).
*   **Removing F:** Breaks P3 (E-F-X-K-G-H-I-J) and P4 (E-F-K-G-H-I-J).
*   **Removing B:** Breaks P5 (E-B-N-Y-O-P-J), P6 (E-B-N-C-P-J), P7 (E-B-C-P-J), P8 (E-B-C-I-J), and P9 (E-B-H-I-J).

All identified paths from E to J are broken by removing the nodes M, F, and B.
Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, and we found a set of 3 nodes that achieves disconnection, this is the minimum.

An alternative cut set of size 3 could be [M, P, I], as all paths to J must pass through M, P, or I (D is a dead end from E's perspective).

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, F, B]

    *   Node X is connected to G, K, and H.
    *   Node K is connected to G, X, H, and F.
    This means that if a path from N reaches X or K, it must have come from H or F (or G, which is the destination). For example, to reach X from N, the path must go through H or K. If it goes through K, K itself is connected to H and F. Therefore, any path from N to G, even if it goes through X or K, must ultimately pass through either H or F to reach the "G-side" of the graph.

4.  **Identify Critical Nodes:** Based on the analysis in step 3, all paths from N to G must pass through either node H or node F. These two nodes act as critical "bridges" connecting the N-side of the graph to the G-side.

5.  **Test Cut Set of Size 1:**
    *   If I remove H, paths like N-B-E-F-G still exist.
    *   If I remove F, paths like N-B-C-H-G still exist.
    *   If I remove any other single node (e.g., E, I, C, B, O, P, J, M, L, D, X, K), there will always be alternative paths remaining between N and G. For instance, removing E still leaves paths like N-B-C-H-G or N-O-P-J-I-H-G. Removing I still leaves paths like N-B-E-F-G or N-B-C-H-G.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient to disconnect N and G. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

6.  **Test Cut Set of Size 2:**
    *   Consider removing both H and F.
        *   All paths that pass through H (e.g., N-B-C-H-G, N-B-C-I-H-G, N-O-P-J-I-H-G, N-O-P-D-J-I-H-G) will be broken.
        *   All paths that pass through F (e.g., N-B-E-F-G, N-O-P-J-M-L-E-F-G, N-O-P-D-J-M-L-E-F-G) will be broken.
        *   Since all paths from N to G must pass through either H or F (as established in step 4), removing both H and F will ensure that no path remains between N and G.
        *   After removing H and F, G is still connected to X and K, and X is connected to K. However, N cannot reach X or K without passing through H or F. For example, to reach X or K, N would need to connect via H or F, which are now removed. Nodes like E, I, C, B, O, P, J, M, L, D are all on the "N-side" and cannot reach X or K without H or F.

7.  **Conclusion:** Removing nodes H and F successfully disconnects N from G, and no smaller set of nodes can achieve this.

Image name: POGBa_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are G and I. My goal is to disconnect G from I by removing the minimum number of white nodes.

2.  **Explore Paths from G to I:** I will systematically identify distinct paths between G and I. To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for node-disjoint paths. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them.

    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I
        *   Intermediate node: {H}

    *   **Path 2:** Let's find a path that does not use H.
        G - X - K - J - P - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {X, K, J, P}
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (no common intermediate nodes).

    *   **Path 3:** Let's find a path that does not use H, X, K, J, or P.
        From G, I can go to F.
        From F, I can go to E.
        From E, I can go to B.
        From B, I can go to C.
        From C, I can go to I.
        So, Path 3: G - F - E - B - C - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {F, E, B, C}
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (no common intermediate nodes).

3.  **Verify Node-Disjointness:**
    *   Intermediate nodes of Path 1: {H}
    *   Intermediate nodes of Path 2: {X, K, J, P}
    *   Intermediate nodes of Path 3: {F, E, B, C}
    These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually disjoint.

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Since I have found three node-disjoint paths between G and I, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect G and I is 3.

5.  **Identify a Cut Set:** To disconnect G and I, I must remove at least one node from each of these three node-disjoint paths. I can choose any one intermediate node from each path.
    *   From Path 1: H
    *   From Path 2: J (could also be X, K, or P)
    *   From Path 3: E (could also be F, B, or C)

    Therefore, a valid cut set is [H, J, E].

Step-by-step reasoning.

2.  **Analyze connections of target nodes:**
    *   Node H is connected to G and I.
    *   Node B is connected to E, G, C, and N.

3.  **Consider paths from H to B:**
    Any path originating from H must start with either the edge (H, G) or the edge (H, I).

    *   **Paths starting with (H, G):**
        *   H - G - B
        *   H - G - E - B
        *   H - G - F - ... (no path to B from F without going through G or E)
        *   H - G - K - ... (no path to B from K)

    *   **Paths starting with (H, I):**
        *   H - I - C - B
        *   H - I - C - N - B
        *   H - I - P - O - C - B
        *   H - I - P - O - N - B
        *   H - I - J - P - O - C - B (longer path)
        *   H - I - J - P - O - N - B (longer path)

4.  **Determine a minimal cut set:**
    To disconnect H from B, all paths between them must be broken. Since all paths from H must use either (H, G) or (H, I) as their first edge, removing both of these edges will completely isolate H from the rest of the graph. If H is isolated, no path can exist between H and B.

    *   Removing (H, G) breaks all paths that start H-G...
    *   Removing (H, I) breaks all paths that start H-I...

    Therefore, the set of edges {(H, G), (H, I)} will disconnect H from B.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    Node H has a degree of 2 (it is connected to exactly two other nodes: G and I). To completely disconnect H from the rest of the graph, at least two edges must be removed.
    *   If only one edge is removed (e.g., (H, G)), H would still be connected to I, and paths like H-I-C-B would still exist.
    *   If only one edge is removed (e.g., (H, I)), H would still be connected to G, and paths like H-G-B would still exist.
    Since removing two edges (H, G) and (H, I) achieves disconnection, and one edge is not sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, H), (H, I)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and E. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and E.

First, I identify all direct connections to the target node E. Node E is connected to B, P, L, and M. Any path from H to E must use one of these four edges as its final step: (E, B), (E, P), (E, L), or (E, M).

Let's analyze the paths from H to E by considering these "entry points" to E:

1.  **Paths from H to E via B:**
    *   H can reach B directly (H-B-E).
    *   H can reach B via C (H-C-B-E).
    *   H can reach B via N (H-N-B-E).
    *   All these paths converge at B before reaching E. Removing the edge **(B, E)** would disconnect all paths from H that pass through B to reach E.

2.  **Paths from H to E via P:**
    *   H can reach P via I (H-I-P-E).
    *   H can reach P via C and I (H-C-I-P-E).
    *   H can reach P via G and F (H-G-F-P-E).
    *   H can reach P via O (H-O-P-E).
    *   H can reach P via J (H-I-J-P-E).
    *   All these paths converge at P before reaching E. Removing the edge **(P, E)** would disconnect all paths from H that pass through P to reach E.

3.  **Paths from H to E via M (and L):**
    *   E is connected to M via (E, M). M is connected to J, L.
    *   E is connected to L via (E, L). L is connected to M.
    *   To reach M or L from H, one must pass through J (since M and L are only connected to E and J/M respectively, and E is the target).
    *   H can reach J via I (H-I-J).
    *   So, paths are like H - ... - I - J - M - E and H - ... - I - J - M - L - E.
    *   To cut all paths from H to E that go through M or L, we can remove the edge **(J, M)**. This would prevent H from reaching M (and consequently L) via J. Since M and L have no other connections to the H-side of the graph apart from J, this single edge removal effectively cuts all paths through M and L.

By removing the three edges **(B, E)**, **(P, E)**, and **(J, M)**, we ensure the following:
*   **(B, E)** disconnects all paths from H that would use node B to reach E.
*   **(P, E)** disconnects all paths from H that would use node P to reach E.
*   **(J, M)** disconnects all paths from H that would use nodes M or L to reach E, as J is the only gateway from the H-side to M (and then L).

Let's verify that no path remains:
*   Any path from H to E must end with one of (B,E), (P,E), (M,E), or (L,E).
*   (B,E) is removed.
*   (P,E) is removed.
*   If a path were to use (M,E), it would have to reach M first. The only way to reach M from H (without going through E) is via J (H -> ... -> J -> M). Since (J,M) is removed, M is unreachable from H.
*   If a path were to use (L,E), it would have to reach L first. The only way to reach L from H (without going through E) is via M (H -> ... -> M -> L). Since M is unreachable from H, L is also unreachable.

Therefore, these three edges successfully disconnect H from E.

Now, I need to ensure this is the *minimum* number of edges.
If we remove only two edges, say:
1.  **(B, E)** and **(P, E)**: A path like H-I-J-M-E would still exist.
2.  **(B, E)** and **(J, M)**: A path like H-I-P-E would still exist.
3.  **(P, E)** and **(J, M)**: A path like H-B-E would still exist.

Since removing any two edges leaves at least one path, the minimum number of edges required is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, E), (J, M), (P, E)]

Image name: POGBb_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect N from H. I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between N and H.**
    I will systematically trace paths from N to H.

2.  **Find edge-disjoint paths.**
    *   **Path 1:** A direct path from N to H is:
        N - B - H
        Edges in Path 1: `(N, B)`, `(B, H)`

    *   **Path 2:** Now, I look for a path from N to H that does not use any edges from Path 1 (`(N, B)`, `(B, H)`).
        N - O - C - I - H
        Edges in Path 2: `(N, O)`, `(O, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, H)`
        These two paths are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** Next, I search for a third path from N to H that does not use any edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        Edges used so far: `(N, B)`, `(B, H)`, `(N, O)`, `(O, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, H)`.
        Starting from N, the only remaining unused outgoing edge is `(N, Y)`.
        From Y, I can go to O. The edge `(Y, O)` is not used.
        From O, I can go to P. The edge `(O, P)` is not used.
        From P, I can go to D. The edge `(P, D)` is not used.
        From D, I can go to J. The edge `(D, J)` is not used.
        From J, I can go to M. The edge `(J, M)` is not used.
        From M, I can go to L. The edge `(M, L)` is not used.
        From L, I can go to E. The edge `(L, E)` is not used.
        From E, I can go to F. The edge `(E, F)` is not used.
        From F, I can go to X. The edge `(F, X)` is not used.
        From X, I can go to G. The edge `(X, G)` is not used.
        From G, I can go to H. The edge `(G, H)` is not used.
        So, Path 3 is:
        N - Y - O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - X - G - H
        Edges in Path 3: `(N, Y)`, `(Y, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, D)`, `(D, J)`, `(J, M)`, `(M, L)`, `(L, E)`, `(E, F)`, `(F, X)`, `(X, G)`, `(G, H)`

3.  **Verify edge-disjointness of all three paths.**
    *   Path 1: `(N, B)`, `(B, H)`
    *   Path 2: `(N, O)`, `(O, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, H)`
    *   Path 3: `(N, Y)`, `(Y, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, D)`, `(D, J)`, `(J, M)`, `(M, L)`, `(L, E)`, `(E, F)`, `(F, X)`, `(X, G)`, `(G, H)`
    Upon careful inspection, no edge is shared between any two of these three paths. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges to remove.**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect N and H is 3.

5.  **Identify a minimal cut set.**
    A straightforward way to disconnect H from N is to remove all edges directly connected to H that are part of these edge-disjoint paths.
    *   ` (B, H)` (cuts Path 1)
    *   ` (I, H)` (cuts Path 2)
    *   ` (G, H)` (cuts Path 3)

    *   N-side: {N}
    *   G-side: {G}

    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J. Edges: {(E, M), (M, J)}
    *   **Path 2:** E - P - J. Edges: {(E, P), (P, J)}
    *   **Path 3:** E - B - I - J. Edges: {(E, B), (B, I), (I, J)}

    Let's check if these paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   (E, M) is unique to Path 1.
    *   (M, J) is unique to Path 1.
    *   (E, P) is unique to Path 2.
    *   (P, J) is unique to Path 2.
    *   (E, B) is unique to Path 3.
    *   (B, I) is unique to Path 3.
    *   (I, J) is unique to Path 3.
    Indeed, these three paths are edge-disjoint.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. We have found a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are J and E. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between J and E.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node J (black) is connected to D, P, M, I.
    *   Node E (black) is connected to B, L, F.

2.  **Analyze paths from J to E:**
    Any path from J to E must eventually pass through one of E's direct neighbors: B, L, or F. Let's consider these three "entry points" to E.

    *   **Path through L:** The only way to reach L from J is via M (J - M - L). So, any path from J to E via L must use the sequence J - M - L - E.
    *   **Path through B:** There are multiple paths from J to B (e.g., J - I - C - B, J - M - O - N - B, etc.). All these paths converge at B, and then connect to E via the edge (B, E).
    *   **Path through F:** There are multiple paths from J to F (e.g., J - I - G - F, J - I - H - X - F, etc.). Additionally, paths can go through B to F (e.g., J - ... - B - F). All these paths eventually reach F, and then connect to E via the edge (F, E).

3.  **Identify critical edges for disconnection:**
    To completely disconnect J from E, we must ensure that no path can reach E through any of its direct neighbors (B, L, or F). This means we need to cut at least one edge from each of these "branches" leading to E.

    *   To cut the branch through L: We must remove either (M, L) or (E, L).
    *   To cut the branch through B: We must remove the edge (B, E), as it's the only direct connection from B to E.
    *   To cut the branch through F: We must remove the edge (F, E), as it's the only direct connection from F to E.

4.  **Formulate a minimal cut set:**
    Based on the above, we must remove (B, E) and (F, E). For the L branch, we can choose to remove (E, L) (the edge directly connected to E) or (M, L). Choosing (E, L) results in a cut set where all removed edges are directly connected to E.

    Therefore, a minimal set of edges to remove is:
    *   (E, B) - to cut all paths through B.
    *   (E, L) - to cut all paths through L.
    *   (E, F) - to cut all paths through F.

    This set has 3 edges.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   Can we disconnect J and E by removing only 1 edge? No, because removing any single edge (E, B), (E, L), or (E, F) would leave at least two other paths to E open.
    *   Can we disconnect J and E by removing only 2 edges?
        *   If we remove (E, B) and (E, L), paths through F (e.g., J - I - G - F - E) still exist.
        *   If we remove (E, B) and (E, F), paths through L (e.g., J - M - L - E) still exist.
        *   If we remove (E, L) and (E, F), paths through B (e.g., J - I - C - B - E) still exist.
        *   Therefore, removing any two edges directly connected to E is insufficient.
        *   It's also unlikely that two edges not directly connected to E could cut all three distinct branches (L, B, F) more efficiently. For example, cutting (M, L) and (N, B) would still leave paths through C to B (J - I - C - B - E) and all paths through F (J - I - G - F - E).

    Since 1 or 2 edges are not sufficient, and we found a set of 3 edges that works, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, B), (E, L), (E, F)]

        *   Intermediate nodes: {Y, O}

    *   D is connected to J and P. Since J is removed, D is now only connected to P.
    *   All paths from N to P must pass through I, O, or J (or through D, which itself relies on J to be reached from N).
    *   Since I, O, and J are removed, N can no longer reach any of P's direct neighbors (I, O, J) or any node that would lead to D (as D is only reachable from N via J).
    *   Therefore, N is completely disconnected from P.

8.  **Conclusion:** We found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, meaning the minimum cut size is at least 3. We also found a cut set of size 3 ({I, O, J}) that successfully disconnects N and P. Thus, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

    *   Paths starting with F-G:
        *   F - G - H - I - J
        *   F - G - H - I - P - J
        *   F - G - H - B - I - J
        *   F - G - H - B - C - I - J
        *   F - G - H - B - C - N - O - P - J
    *   Paths starting with F-K or F-X:
        Nodes F, K, X, G form a complete subgraph (K4), meaning they are all directly connected to each other. Any path from F that goes through K or X must eventually pass through G to reach the rest of the graph towards J (since E is the only other direct connection from F, and K, X are not directly connected to H, B, C, I, M, P, D, J).
        *   F - K - G - H - ... (then any of the G-H paths above)
        *   F - K - X - G - H - ... (then any of the G-H paths above)
        *   F - X - G - H - ... (then any of the G-H paths above)
        *   F - X - K - G - H - ... (then any of the G-H paths above)

2.  **Identify potential bottleneck edges:**
    *   All paths from F to J must either start with the edge (F, E) or pass through the node G (either directly via (F,G) or indirectly via (F,K) or (F,X) and then (K,G) or (X,G)).
    *   If a path goes through G, it must then proceed towards J. Looking at the neighbors of G (F, K, X, H), and knowing F, K, X are on F's "side" of the graph, the only edge from G that leads towards J is (G, H). Therefore, all paths from F that pass through G must use the edge (G, H).

3.  **Propose a cut set based on bottlenecks:**
    Based on the observation above, we can propose a cut set of two edges:
    *   (F, E): This edge cuts off all paths that start from F through E.
    *   (G, H): This edge cuts off all paths that start from F through G (including those that go F-K-G, F-X-G, F-K-X-G, F-X-K-G, etc., because G is the only gateway from {F,K,X} to H and beyond towards J).

4.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    Let's assume edges (F, E) and (G, H) are removed.
    *   **Paths starting F-E:** All paths like F-E-M-J, F-E-I-J, F-E-I-P-J are broken because the edge (F, E) is removed.
    *   **Paths starting F-G:** All paths like F-G-H-I-J, F-G-H-B-C-N-O-P-J, etc., are broken because the edge (G, H) is removed.
    *   **Paths starting F-K or F-X:**
        *   A path like F-K-G-H-I-J: The segment F-K-G is still intact, but to proceed towards J, it must use (G, H), which is removed. So, this path is broken.
        *   A path like F-K-X-G-H-I-J: The segment F-K-X-G is still intact, but to proceed towards J, it must use (G, H), which is removed. So, this path is broken.
        *   Similarly for F-X-G-... and F-X-K-G-.... All these paths are broken because they rely on (G, H) to move past G towards J.

    Since all possible paths from F to J are broken by removing (F, E) and (G, H), this is a valid cut set.

5.  **Determine minimality:**
    The cut set has a size of 2. To prove it's minimal, we would typically show that there are at least two edge-disjoint paths.
    *   Path 1: F - E - M - J
    *   Path 2: F - G - H - I - J
    These two paths are edge-disjoint. This confirms that at least 2 edges must be removed. Since we found a cut set of size 2, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, E), (G, H)]

4.  **Determine the minimum cut set:**
    To completely disconnect M from B, we must break all paths identified in step 3.
    *   To break the paths in the "M - E" group (Path 1 and Path 2), we remove the edge (E, B).
    *   To break all paths in the "M - J" group, we remove the edge (M, J).

    Therefore, the cut set is [(E, B), (M, J)]. This set contains 2 edges.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   If only one edge is removed, for example (E, B), then paths starting with M-J (e.g., M-J-P-O-N-B) would still exist.
    *   If only one edge is removed, for example (M, J), then paths through E (e.g., M-E-B) would still exist.
    Since removing a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect M and B, and we have found a solution with 2 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, B), (M, J)]

    Let's check if P2 is edge-disjoint from P1:
    -   (M, L) vs (M, E): Disjoint.
    -   (L, E) vs (M, E), (E, B): Disjoint.
    -   (E, F) vs (M, E), (E, B): Disjoint.
    -   (F, G) vs (M, E), (E, B): Disjoint.

*   Removing **J** breaks all paths that connect O's side to H's side via I (as J is the gateway to I from O's side, assuming C is removed).

1.  E - B - C - I - J
2.  E - B - O - N - C - I - J

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and M. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B from M.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node B (black) is connected to: C, E, H, I, N, O.
    *   Node M (black) is connected to: E, J, L.

2.  **Analyze the structure around node M:**
    Node M is directly connected to E, J, and L. Node L is also connected to E. This means any path from B to M must eventually pass through E, J, or L. More specifically, any path to M via L must first pass through E (since L is only connected to E and M, and E is the only node connected to L that can be reached from B). Therefore, all paths from B to M must ultimately pass through E or J.

3.  **Identify potential "bottleneck" edges leading to E and J from the "B-side" of the graph:**
    *   Edges connecting to E from nodes other than M or L: (B, E) and (P, E).
    *   Edges connecting to J from nodes other than M: (P, J) and (D, J). Node D is not connected to B, so (D, J) is not relevant for paths originating from B.

    Therefore, all paths from B to M must pass through at least one of these three edges: (B, E), (P, E), or (P, J).

4.  **Propose a cut set based on these bottleneck edges:**
    If we remove the edges `(B, E)`, `(P, E)`, and `(P, J)`, then:
    *   No path from B can directly reach E via (B, E).
    *   No path from B (or any node on the B-side) can reach E via P, as (P, E) is removed.
    *   No path from B (or any node on the B-side) can reach J via P, as (P, J) is removed.

    Since E and J are the only entry points to the M-side (which includes M and L), removing these three edges would disconnect B from M. This cut set has a size of 3.

5.  **Verify if a smaller cut set (size 1 or 2) is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we can try to find 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and M. If we can find 3 such paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

    Let's find three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** B - E - M
        Edges: `(B, E)`, `(E, M)`
    *   **Path 2:** B - I - P - J - M
        Edges: `(B, I)`, `(I, P)`, `(P, J)`, `(J, M)`
    *   **Path 3:** B - C - O - P - E - L - M
        Edges: `(B, C)`, `(C, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, E)`, `(E, L)`, `(L, M)`

    Let's check if these paths are edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   Path 1 edges: `{(B, E), (E, M)}`
    *   Path 2 edges: `{(B, I), (I, P), (P, J), (J, M)}`
    *   Path 3 edges: `{(B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, E), (E, L), (L, M)}`

    Upon inspection, there are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and M, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them must be at least 3. We also identified a cut set `[(B, E), (P, E), (P, J)]` of size 3 that successfully disconnects B from M. This confirms that the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, E), (P, E), (P, J)]
