First, I will identify all possible paths from E to I.
The nodes directly connected to E are B, F, M, and L.
The nodes directly connected to I are H, C, and P.

Let's list the main "branches" of paths from E:

**Branch 1: Paths starting with E -> B**
*   E - B - H - I
*   E - B - C - I
*   E - B - C - O - P - I
*   E - B - C - N - O - P - I
*   E - B - N - O - P - I

**Branch 2: Paths starting with E -> F**
*   E - F - G - H - I
*   E - F - X - G - H - I
*   E - F - X - K - G - H - I
    *   (Note: All paths from F to I must pass through G, and then from G, the only way to reach I is via H. So, H is a critical node for all paths in this branch.)

**Branch 3: Paths starting with E -> M (or E -> L -> M)**
*   E - M - J - P - I
*   E - M - J - D - P - I
*   E - L - M - J - P - I
*   E - L - M - J - D - P - I
    *   (Note: All paths from M (or L then M) to I must eventually pass through P. So, P is a critical node for all paths in this branch.)

To disconnect E and I, I need to sever all these branches.

**Attempt to find a cut set of size 1:**
*   Removing any single node (e.g., B, F, M, H, C, P) will leave other paths intact. For example, removing B leaves paths through F and M. Removing H leaves paths through C and P. Thus, a single node is not sufficient.

**Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
Let's try combinations of two nodes that seem critical:
*   **[B, H]**:
    *   Removing B breaks all paths in Branch 1.
    *   Removing H breaks all paths in Branch 2 (as H is critical for them).
    *   However, paths in Branch 3 (E-M-J-P-I, etc.) are still intact, as they do not use B or H. So, [B, H] is not a cut set.
*   **[B, P]**:
    *   Removing B breaks all paths in Branch 1.
    *   Removing P breaks all paths in Branch 3 (as P is critical for them).
    *   However, paths in Branch 2 (E-F-G-H-I, etc.) are still intact, as they do not use B or P. So, [B, P] is not a cut set.
*   **[H, P]**:
    *   Removing H breaks all paths in Branch 2.
    *   Removing P breaks all paths in Branch 3.
    *   However, paths in Branch 1 that do not use H or P (e.g., E-B-C-I) are still intact. So, [H, P] is not a cut set.
*   Other combinations of two nodes (e.g., [F, M], [F, H], [M, C], etc.) also leave at least one branch connected. For example, removing F and M leaves all paths in Branch 1 (E-B-...) intact.

Since no combination of two nodes successfully disconnects E and I, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

**Finding a cut set of size 3:**
Let's consider the nodes that are critical for each of the main branches identified:
*   For Branch 1 (paths starting E-B), removing **B** will sever all these paths.
*   For Branch 2 (paths starting E-F), removing **H** will sever all these paths (as H is the common point for all paths from F to I).
*   For Branch 3 (paths starting E-M/L), removing **P** will sever all these paths (as P is the common point for all paths from M/L to I).

Let's verify the cut set **[B, H, P]**:
1.  **Removing B**: All paths starting E-B are broken (e.g., E-B-H-I, E-B-C-I, E-B-C-O-P-I, E-B-C-N-O-P-I, E-B-N-O-P-I).
2.  **Removing H**: All paths starting E-F are broken (e.g., E-F-G-H-I, E-F-X-G-H-I, E-F-X-K-G-H-I).
3.  **Removing P**: All paths starting E-M (or E-L-M) are broken (e.g., E-M-J-P-I, E-M-J-D-P-I, E-L-M-J-P-I, E-L-M-J-D-P-I).

Since all possible initial connections from E (via B, F, or M/L) are blocked by removing B, H, and P respectively, no path remains between E and I. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

**Verification using Menger's Theorem (optional but good for confidence):**
To confirm that 3 is the minimum, I can try to find 3 node-disjoint paths between E and I (paths that share no intermediate nodes).
1.  **Path 1 (P1): E - B - C - I** (Intermediate nodes: {B, C})
2.  **Path 2 (P2): E - F - G - H - I** (Intermediate nodes: {F, G, H})
3.  **Path 3 (P3): E - M - J - P - I** (Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P})
The sets of intermediate nodes {B, C}, {F, G, H}, and {M, J, P} are all disjoint. Therefore, there are 3 node-disjoint paths between E and I. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect E and I is 3.

    *   E - M - J - P - I - H. (Uses I)
    *   E - M - L - (covered by E -> L paths)

*   If a path starts E -> F, it must go through F.
*   If a path starts E -> B, it must go through B.
*   If a path starts E -> I, it must go through I.
*   If a path starts E -> L: The only way to reach H from L is via M -> J -> I -> H or M -> J -> P -> I -> H. Both of these paths require passing through node I.
*   If a path starts E -> M: The only way to reach H from M is via J -> I -> H or J -> P -> I -> H. Both of these paths require passing through node I.

    2.  E - B - H
    3.  E - I - H

    Removing B breaks path 2, but paths 1 and 3 remain.

    3.  Remove {B, I}: Path E - F - G - H still exists.

    *   Node I is connected to: H, J, C, P.

2.  **Analyze all possible paths from G to I and group them by their initial segment from G or their final segment to I:**

    *   **Paths through H:** The most direct path is G-H-I. This path can be cut by removing either edge (G, H) or (H, I).

    *   **Paths through B:** All paths from G that go through node B eventually reach I via nodes C or P. Examples include:
        *   G-B-C-I
        *   G-B-O-C-I
        *   G-B-O-P-I
        *   G-B-N-C-I
        *   G-B-N-Y-O-C-I
        *   G-B-N-Y-O-P-I
        To cut all these paths, we can remove the edge (G, B). Alternatively, we could remove the edges (C, I) and (P, I) (2 edges), or a combination of edges leading from B to C/O/N. Removing (G, B) is the most efficient single edge to cut this entire group of paths.

    *   **Paths through E and F:** All paths from G that go through nodes E or F eventually reach I via node J. Examples include:
        *   G-E-L-M-J-I
        *   G-E-M-J-I
        *   G-F-X-K-J-I
        *   G-F-K-J-I
        To cut all these paths, we can remove the edge (J, I). Alternatively, we could remove (G, E) and (G, F) (2 edges), or a combination of edges leading from E/F to J. Removing (J, I) is the most efficient single edge to cut this entire group of paths.

3.  **Formulate a minimal cut set:**
    Based on the analysis above, we can select one critical edge from each group of paths to ensure all connections between G and I are severed.
    *   To cut paths through H: Remove (H, I).
    *   To cut paths through B: Remove (G, B).
    *   To cut paths through E and F: Remove (J, I).

    This gives us a candidate cut set: [(H, I), (G, B), (J, I)]. This set contains 3 distinct edges.

4.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   Removing (H, I) breaks the direct path G-H-I.
    *   Removing (G, B) breaks all paths originating from G through B (e.g., G-B-C-I, G-B-O-P-I, etc.).
    *   Removing (J, I) breaks all paths that lead to I via J (e.g., G-E-M-J-I, G-F-K-J-I, etc.).
    After removing these three edges, there are no remaining paths from G to I.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    To confirm this is the minimum number of edges, we can consider a vertex cut. If we remove nodes H, B, and J, G becomes disconnected from I.
    *   If H is removed, G is connected to E, F, B. I is connected to J, C, P.
    *   If B is removed, G is connected to E, F, H. I is connected to J, C, P.
    *   If J is removed, G is connected to E, F, H, B. I is connected to H, C, P.
    If all three nodes {H, B, J} are removed:
    *   G is connected to E, F.
    *   I is connected to C, P.
    *   Paths from E (E-L-M) cannot reach I because M is connected to J (which is removed).
    *   Paths from F (F-X-K) cannot reach I because K is connected to J (which is removed).
    Therefore, {H, B, J} is a vertex cut of size 3. By Menger's theorem, the minimum edge cut must be at least equal to the minimum vertex cut. Since we found an edge cut of size 3, and the minimum vertex cut is 3, this edge cut is indeed minimal.

    *   All paths starting with H-I must pass through I.
    *   All paths starting with H-E, H-F, or H-G eventually pass through E. Therefore, E is a critical node for this entire branch of paths.

    *   Removing B breaks all H-B-...-O paths.
    *   Removing I breaks all H-I-...-O paths.
    *   Removing E breaks all H-E-...-O, H-F-E-...-O, and H-G-F-E-...-O paths.
    Thus, {B, I, E} is a cut set of size 3.

4.  **Check for Smaller Cut Sets (Minimum Verification)**:
    *   **Size 1 Cut Set**:
        *   If I remove B, paths through I and E remain (e.g., H-I-C-O, H-E-M-J-P-O).
        *   If I remove I, paths through B and E remain (e.g., H-B-C-O, H-E-M-J-P-O).
        *   If I remove E, paths through B and I remain (e.g., H-B-C-O, H-I-C-O).
        *   If I remove any other single node (e.g., C, N, P, J, M, L, F, G, X, Y, D), multiple paths would still exist.

Image name: PG_task3_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are I (black) and L (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect I from L.

First, I identify all nodes directly connected to L. These are M, E, and F. Any path from I to L must pass through one of these three nodes and then use the edge connecting that node to L.

Let's consider the edges directly connecting to L:
1.  (M, L)
2.  (E, L)
3.  (F, L)

If I remove these three edges, L will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from I. This would be a cut set of size 3. Now, I need to determine if a smaller cut (e.g., of size 1 or 2) is possible.

To prove that 3 is the minimum, I will try to find three edge-disjoint paths between I and L. If I can find three such paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

Let's search for edge-disjoint paths:

**Path 1:** I can see a direct path from I to J, then to M, and finally to L.
*   P1: I - J - M - L
*   Edges: (I, J), (J, M), (M, L)

**Path 2:** I can see a path from I to C, then to B, then to E, and finally to L.
*   P2: I - C - B - E - L
*   Edges: (I, C), (C, B), (B, E), (E, L)

**Path 3:** I can see a path from I to H, then to G, then to F, and finally to L.
*   P3: I - H - G - F - L
*   Edges: (I, H), (H, G), (G, F), (F, L)

Now, I need to verify if these three paths are truly edge-disjoint (meaning they do not share any common edges).
*   Edges in P1: {(I, J), (J, M), (M, L)}
*   Edges in P2: {(I, C), (C, B), (B, E), (E, L)}
*   Edges in P3: {(I, H), (H, G), (G, F), (F, L)}

Let's compare the edge sets:
*   (I, J) is unique to P1. (I, C) is unique to P2. (I, H) is unique to P3.
*   (J, M) is unique to P1. (C, B) is unique to P2. (H, G) is unique to P3.
*   (M, L) is unique to P1. (B, E) is unique to P2. (G, F) is unique to P3.
*   (E, L) is unique to P2. (F, L) is unique to P3.

All edges in these three paths are distinct. Therefore, I have successfully identified three edge-disjoint paths between I and L.

According to Menger's theorem, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them. Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

I already identified a cut set of size 3: [(M, L), (E, L), (F, L)]. This set of edges effectively disconnects L from all incoming paths. Since I found a cut set of size 3 and proved that the minimum cut must be at least 3, this cut set is indeed minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, L), (E, L), (F, L)]

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from N.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between H and N.**
    To systematically approach this, I will look for edge-disjoint paths. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

    Let's find three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: `(H, B)`, `(B, N)`

    *   **Path 2:** H - G - E - B - C - N
        Edges: `(H, G)`, `(G, E)`, `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, N)`

    *   **Path 3:** H - I - C - Y - N
        Edges: `(H, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, Y)`, `(Y, N)`

2.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths.**
    Let's list all edges used in these three paths:
    *   From Path 1: `(H, B)`, `(B, N)`
    *   From Path 2: `(H, G)`, `(G, E)`, `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, N)`
    *   From Path 3: `(H, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, Y)`, `(Y, N)`

    Upon inspection, all edges listed above are unique and do not overlap between the three paths. For example, `(H, B)` is only in Path 1, `(H, G)` is only in Path 2, and `(H, I)` is only in Path 3. Similarly, `(B, N)` is only in Path 1, `(C, N)` is only in Path 2, and `(Y, N)` is only in Path 3. The intermediate edges are also distinct.

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem.**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges required to disconnect H from N is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3.**
    To confirm that 3 is indeed the minimum, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects H and N. A straightforward way to do this is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.

    Node N is connected to three other nodes: B, C, and Y.
    The edges connected to N are: `(N, B)`, `(N, C)`, and `(N, Y)`.

    If these three edges are removed, node N becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between H and N. This set of edges has a size of 3.

    Alternatively, I could remove all edges connected to H: `(H, B)`, `(H, G)`, `(H, I)`. This would also isolate H and disconnect it from N, and it also has a size of 3.

5.  **Conclusion.**
    Since we have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths (meaning at least 3 edges must be removed) and found a cut set of 3 edges that achieves disconnection, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)]

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal

Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and H. This is a minimum (s,t)-cut problem, and by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H.

First, I will identify all possible paths between E and H and then try to find a set of edge-disjoint paths.

Let's list potential paths from E to H:
1.  **P1: E - H** (Direct connection)
    Edges: `(E, H)`

2.  **P2: E - F - H**
    Edges: `(E, F), (F, H)`

3.  **P3: E - G - H**
    Edges: `(E, G), (G, H)`

These three paths (P1, P2, P3) are clearly edge-disjoint from each other, as they use distinct edges connected to E and H (or the direct edge).

Now, I need to find more paths that are edge-disjoint from P1, P2, P3, and from each other.
The edges used so far are: `(E, H), (E, F), (F, H), (E, G), (G, H)`.

Let's look at the remaining connections from E: `(E, L), (E, M)`.
Let's look at the remaining connections to H: `(H, B), (H, I)`.

4.  **P4: E - M - J - I - H**
    Edges: `(E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)`
    Let's check if these edges are disjoint from P1, P2, P3:
    - `(E, M)` is new.
    - `(M, J)` is new.
    - `(J, I)` is new.
    - `(I, H)` is new.
    So, P4 is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, P3.

Now, I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths:
P1: `(E, H)`
P2: `(E, F), (F, H)`
P3: `(E, G), (G, H)`
P4: `(E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)`

Let's try to find a 5th edge-disjoint path.
The edges used so far are: `(E, H), (E, F), (F, H), (E, G), (G, H), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)`.

Remaining connections from E: `(E, L)`.
Remaining connections to H: `(H, B)`.

Let's try to construct a path using `(E, L)` and `(H, B)`:
Start from E: `E - L` (edge `(E, L)` is available).
From L: `L - M` (edge `(L, M)` is available).
From M: `M - J` (edge `(M, J)` is **NOT available**, as it was used in P4).

Since any path from E through L must pass through M and then J (as M is only connected to L and J, and L is only connected to E and M), and the edge `(M, J)` is already used by P4, I cannot find a 5th edge-disjoint path.

Therefore, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H is 4.
By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and H is 4.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that disconnects E and H.
A good strategy is to identify "bottleneck" edges that cut multiple paths.
Consider the edges that are the "last" connections to H for the paths identified, or critical edges that cut multiple paths.

Let's consider the following cut set:
1.  `(E, H)`: This cuts the direct path P1.
2.  `(F, H)`: This cuts path P2 (E-F-H).
3.  `(G, H)`: This cuts path P3 (E-G-H).
4.  `(M, J)`: This edge is crucial because it is part of P4 (E-M-J-I-H). It also serves as a bottleneck for any path from E that goes through M or L (e.g., E-L-M-J-...).

Let's verify if removing these 4 edges `{(E, H), (F, H), (G, H), (M, J)}` disconnects E and H:
*   **Direct path E-H**: Broken by removing `(E, H)`.
*   **Paths via F**: E-F-H. Broken by removing `(F, H)`.
*   **Paths via G**: E-G-H. Broken by removing `(G, H)`.
*   **Paths via M**: Any path starting E-M... must pass through `(M, J)` to continue towards H (e.g., E-M-J-I-H, E-M-J-P-O-N-B-H). All these paths are broken by removing `(M, J)`.
*   **Paths via L**: Any path starting E-L... must pass through `(L, M)` and then `(M, J)` to continue towards H (e.g., E-L-M-J-I-H, E-L-M-J-P-O-N-B-H). All these paths are broken by removing `(M, J)`.

Since all identified paths (and any other possible path) are broken by the removal of these 4 edges, this cut set is valid. As we determined that 4 is the minimum number of edges required, this is the minimal cut set.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (F, H), (G, H), (M, J)]

    *   **Path A:** X - G - B - C (Intermediate nodes: {G, B})
    *   **Path B:** X - F - E - L - M - J - P - I - C (Intermediate nodes: {F, E, L, M, J, P, I})

    These two paths (Path A and Path B) are vertex-disjoint. Since I found two vertex-disjoint paths, at least two nodes must be removed to disconnect X and C. This confirms that the minimum cut set size is at least 2.

6.  **Propose a Cut Set of Size 2:**
    Based on the path analysis, I observe that all paths from X either go through node E (from F) or through node G (from F, K, or directly from X).

    *   All paths from P to N are indeed broken by removing {O, I, B}.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F and M. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F from M.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node F (black): Connected to K, X, G, E.
    *   Node M (black): Connected to L, E, J.

2.  **Determine an upper bound for the cut size:**
    *   To disconnect M, I could remove all edges connected to it: (M, L), (M, E), (M, J). This would be 3 edges.
    *   To disconnect F, I could remove all edges connected to it: (F, K), (F, X), (F, G), (F, E). This would be 4 edges.
    *   So, the minimum number of edges is at most 3.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths between F and M:**
    According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (min-cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then the min-cut is at least *k*.

    Let's find edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** F - E - M
        Edges used: (F, E), (E, M)

    *   **Path 2:** F - G - H - I - J - M
        Edges used: (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J), (J, M)
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses entirely different edges.

    Since I have found two edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

4.  **Identify a cut set of size 2:**
    Now that I know the minimum cut is at least 2, I will try to find a cut set with exactly 2 edges.
    Consider the "left" cluster of nodes around F: {F, K, X, G}.
    Let's identify all edges that connect this cluster to the rest of the graph.
    *   From F: (F, E) connects to E (which is outside the cluster).
    *   From G: (G, H) connects to H (which is outside the cluster).
    *   Nodes K and X are only connected to F and G, which are within this cluster.

    Therefore, all paths from the {F, K, X, G} cluster to the rest of the graph (which includes M) must pass through either edge (F, E) or edge (G, H).

    Let's propose the cut set: **[(F, E), (G, H)]**.

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   **Removal of (F, E):** This breaks any direct path from F to E, and thus any path that starts F-E-...-M.
    *   **Removal of (G, H):** This breaks any path from G to H, and thus any path that starts F-G-H-...-M, or F-K-G-H-...-M, or F-X-G-H-...-M.

    After removing these two edges:
    *   Node F can only reach K, X, and G.
    *   Node G can only reach F, K, and X.
    *   The entire cluster {F, K, X, G} is now isolated from the rest of the graph.
    *   Since M is not part of this cluster, F is completely disconnected from M.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    I have found a cut set of 2 edges that disconnects F and M. Since I previously determined that the minimum cut must be at least 2 (due to two edge-disjoint paths), this cut set is indeed minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, E), (G, H)]
